In 2010, Florida amended its hate crime laws to include homeless status as a protected category, recognizing the increasing violence against homeless individuals. citeturn0search2 Despite this legal protection, enforcement remains inconsistent, and many attacks go unreported or unaddressed.

The criminalization of homelessness exacerbates these issues.

Laws and ordinances that prohibit sleeping in public spaces, panhandling, and other life-sustaining activities effectively punish individuals for their homelessness.

This approach not only fails to address the root causes of homelessness but also perpetuates a cycle of marginalization and abuse.

We call upon law enforcement agencies, policymakers, and the community at large to recognize and address the systemic issues contributing to the mistreatment of homeless individuals.

It is imperative to enforce existing hate crime laws that include homeless status, provide adequate training for law enforcement on interacting with homeless populations, and implement policies that focus on support and rehabilitation rather than punishment.

Our society must move beyond viewing homelessness as a crime or a personal failing and instead address it as the complex social issue it is, deserving of compassion, support, and actionable solutions.

This is our call to you—to stand with us, to advocate for us, and to ensure that the rights and dignity of all individuals, housed or unhoused, are upheld and respected.

Here's an explanation based on Florida law: --- ### **Grounds for a Civil Suit Against Miami-Dade County** 1. **Negligence of Law Enforcement (Florida Statutes § 768.28 - Sovereign Immunity)** Under Florida law, sovereign immunity limits the liability of government entities, but exceptions exist for negligence in operational functions.

Miami-Dade County, through its law enforcement officers, had a duty to act reasonably and protect the public from foreseeable harm.

Their failure to: - Arrest Gabriel Calderon after a documented and unprovoked violent attack, - Adequately investigate the incident, and - Provide proper follow-up to prevent subsequent harassment, constitutes gross negligence under § 768.28, which waives sovereign immunity for negligent acts committed by government employees within the scope of their employment.

The repeated failure of the Miami-Dade Police Department to address our reports demonstrates a clear breach of duty, leading to ongoing harm, fear, and emotional distress. 2. **Violation of Equal Protection Rights (Florida Constitution, Article I, Section 2)** Article I, Section 2 of the Florida Constitution guarantees equal protection under the law.

The systemic discrimination we experienced—based on our homeless status—violates this principle.

The apparent disregard by law enforcement for cases involving homeless individuals, as evidenced by the police’s refusal to act on our repeated reports, constitutes discriminatory practices.

The police's use of the acronym "NHI" (No Humans Involved) further demonstrates their systemic bias and dehumanization of individuals like us, violating our fundamental rights under state law. 3. **Civil Rights Violations (Florida Statutes § 760.01 - Florida Civil Rights Act)** Florida’s Civil Rights Act prohibits discrimination based on protected characteristics and includes a broader interpretation of discriminatory conduct by government entities.

While "homeless status" is not explicitly listed, the targeted neglect and refusal to protect us based on our status as homeless individuals could constitute a violation of the Act under its anti-discriminatory principles. 4. **Intentional Infliction of Emotional Distress (Common Law Tort Claim)** The inaction and deliberate indifference of Miami-Dade County have caused us severe emotional distress.

Courts have recognized that intentional or reckless conduct resulting in extreme emotional harm is actionable under tort law.

In our case, the County’s repeated failure to act, despite clear evidence of escalating threats and violence, meets the threshold for reckless disregard of our safety and emotional well-being. 5. **Failure to Protect (DeShaney v.

Supreme Court ruled in *DeShaney* that government entities are generally not liable for failing to protect individuals from harm, exceptions arise when a "special relationship" exists or when the government’s actions create or exacerbate the danger.

In our case, law enforcement’s repeated refusal to act on our reports effectively emboldened Gabriel Calderon, creating an environment where he felt untouchable and could continue harassing us.

This places liability squarely on the County for facilitating the danger. --- ### **Why Miami-Dade County is Liable** - **Gross Negligence**: Police failed to arrest Gabriel Calderon or properly investigate the incident, despite overwhelming evidence, leaving us vulnerable to repeated harassment and violence. - **Discrimination**: Systemic bias against homeless individuals denied us equal protection under the law and perpetuated harm. - **Emotional Harm**: Their deliberate indifference caused ongoing emotional distress, fear, and suffering. - **Failure to Uphold Duty**: Law enforcement and related agencies failed in their duty to protect us, prioritizing prejudice over public safety. --- This combination of statutory violations, constitutional breaches, and common law claims provides a strong foundation for a civil suit against Miami-Dade County. > **User (2024-12-27 20:57:33 UTC):** > > Please write me an email to the ACLU explaining all the injustices we’ve endured throughout this debacle and that we urgently need assistance in holding Miami-Dade responsible for its betrayal against the homeless.

In fact, national statistics reveal a troubling trend of rising homelessness, with many cities struggling to address this growing crisis.

While many factors contribute to this problem, the criminalization of homelessness is a growing concern that exacerbates the challenges faced by those living on the streets.

This article will explore their perspectives and offer concrete steps to combat the hate and criminalization of homeless people, drawing on research into successful programs and initiatives that have effectively reduced homelessness and improved the lives of individuals and families. > Celebrity Advocates for the Homeless > Several prominent figures have used their platforms to raise awareness about the injustices faced by homeless individuals and advocate for their rights.

Here are a few examples: > • Jon Bon Jovi: A long-time advocate for the homeless, Jon Bon Jovi has established the Jon Bon Jovi Soul Foundation, which has built over 600 affordable housing units in 10 states.

He also opened the JBJ Soul Kitchen, a community restaurant where diners can pay for their meals by volunteering or donating, ensuring everyone has access to a hot meal2. > • Miley Cyrus: Deeply moved by her visit to a homeless youth shelter in Los Angeles, Miley Cyrus used her acceptance speech at the 2014 MTV Video Music Awards to raise awareness about youth homelessness.

Their efforts demonstrate the power of using one's platform to advocate for change and inspire others to get involved. > Understanding the Root Causes of Homelessness > To effectively address homelessness, it's crucial to understand the underlying factors that contribute to it.

These root causes are multifaceted and often interconnected, including: > • Lack of Affordable Housing: The increasing cost of housing, coupled with stagnant wages, makes it difficult for low-income individuals and families to find safe and stable housing.

In many cities, the average rent for a modest apartment requires an income far exceeding the minimum wage, leaving many struggling to make ends meet10. > • Poverty: Poverty is a significant driver of homelessness, as individuals and families struggle to meet their basic needs, including housing, food, and healthcare.

The lack of a financial safety net can quickly lead to homelessness when unexpected expenses arise or when individuals face job loss or health crises10. > • Unemployment: Job loss or lack of employment opportunities can lead to financial instability and increase the risk of homelessness.

Without a steady income, individuals and families may struggle to pay rent, utilities, and other essential expenses, ultimately leading to eviction and homelessness10. > • Lack of Affordable Healthcare: High healthcare costs and lack of access to affordable healthcare can create a financial burden that pushes individuals and families into homelessness.

A single medical emergency can result in insurmountable debt, forcing individuals to choose between healthcare and housing10. > • Domestic Violence: Domestic violence is a significant contributor to homelessness, particularly for women and families fleeing abusive situations.

In fact, a staggering percentage of domestic violence victims who received housing services from domestic violence programs, which includes emergency shelters and transitional housing, did so in a 24-hour period12.

This highlights the urgent need for safe and affordable housing options for those escaping violence. > • Mental Health and Addiction Challenges: Mental health issues and substance abuse can exacerbate the challenges faced by homeless individuals and make it more difficult to access support and maintain stable housing.

These issues can be both a cause and a consequence of homelessness, creating a vicious cycle that is difficult to break without proper support and resources12. > Addressing these root causes is essential to creating long-term solutions to homelessness and preventing individuals and families from falling into homelessness in the first place. > The Harmful Impact of Criminalizing Homelessness > Criminalizing homelessness involves enacting laws and policies that penalize activities associated with homelessness, such as sleeping in public, panhandling, and loitering.

These measures often result in fines, arrests, and incarceration, further marginalizing and harming those experiencing homelessness.

The criminalization of homelessness has several detrimental effects: > • It creates a cycle of poverty and homelessness.

Fines and criminal records make it more difficult for individuals to find employment, secure housing, and access essential services, trapping them in a cycle of poverty and homelessness14. > • It exacerbates mental health and addiction issues.

Enforcing laws that criminalize homelessness is costly and ineffective.

The funds spent on policing and incarceration could be better used to provide housing and support services. > • It violates human rights.

Criminalizing basic human needs like sleeping and seeking shelter is a violation of fundamental human rights. > • It disproportionately impacts people of color.

The criminalization of homelessness perpetuates systemic racism, as these laws are often enforced more aggressively in communities of color.

This results in higher rates of arrest and incarceration for people of color experiencing homelessness, further marginalizing and disenfranchising these communities15. > Organizations Fighting to Decriminalize Homelessness > Several organizations are working tirelessly to decriminalize homelessness and advocate for more humane and effective solutions.

These organizations provide legal assistance, advocacy, and support to homeless individuals and communities: > • National Alliance to End Homelessness: This nonpartisan organization works to prevent and end homelessness in the United States by advocating for policy changes, providing resources, and supporting community-based solutions17. > • National Coalition for the Homeless: This national organization works to end homelessness by addressing the underlying causes, protecting the civil rights of homeless individuals, and advocating for policies that prioritize housing and support services15. > • Housing Not Handcuffs: This national campaign focuses on ending the criminalization of homelessness by advocating for policies that prioritize housing and support services over law enforcement18. > • Amnesty International: This global human rights organization advocates for the decriminalization of homelessness and poverty, recognizing that criminalizing basic human needs is a violation of fundamental rights14. > • American Bar Association Commission on Homelessness & Poverty: This commission works to address homelessness and poverty through legal advocacy, policy development, and community engagement16. > These organizations play a vital role in challenging the criminalization of homelessness and promoting solutions that prioritize human dignity and well-being. > Proven Solutions to Reduce Homelessness > Ending homelessness requires a multi-pronged approach that addresses the root causes and provides comprehensive support to those in need.

Here are some proven solutions that have been successful in reducing homelessness: > • Housing First: This approach prioritizes providing permanent housing to individuals experiencing homelessness without requiring them to meet preconditions like sobriety or employment.

Studies have shown that Housing First is highly effective in reducing chronic homelessness19. > • Permanent Supportive Housing: This model combines affordable housing with wraparound support services, such as case management, mental health care, and substance abuse treatment, to help individuals maintain stable housing and address underlying issues.

To prioritize people experiencing chronic homelessness in existing supportive housing programs, communities can: 19 > • Establish a limited preference for people experiencing chronic homelessness when allocating Housing Choice vouchers, public housing units, and multifamily housing units. > • Use project-based Housing Choice vouchers within apartment buildings being developed to serve as supportive housing. > • Increase opportunities for tenants of supportive housing who no longer need intensive services to "move up" through Housing Choice vouchers and other affordable housing and employment services, freeing up supportive housing opportunities for people experiencing chronic homelessness. > • Homelessness Prevention Programs: These programs provide financial assistance, legal aid, and other support services to individuals and families at risk of homelessness to prevent them from losing their housing20. > • Emergency Solutions Grants: These federal grants provide funding for street outreach, emergency shelter, and rapid re-housing programs to address the immediate needs of individuals experiencing homelessness22. > • Runaway and Homeless Youth Programs: These programs provide specialized services to youth experiencing homelessness, including street outreach, emergency shelters, and transitional living programs23. > These solutions demonstrate that investing in housing and support services is more effective and humane than criminalizing homelessness. > Taking Action: How to Stop the Hate > Ending the hate and criminalization of homeless people requires a collective effort from individuals, communities, and policymakers.

Here are some actionable steps you can take: > • Educate Yourself: Learn about the root causes of homelessness, the harmful effects of criminalization, and the proven solutions that work.

Read articles, reports, and books on homelessness, and attend workshops or conferences to deepen your understanding. > • Advocate for Policy Change: Contact your elected officials and urge them to support policies that prioritize housing and support services over criminalization.

Support legislation that promotes affordable housing, expands access to healthcare, and addresses the underlying causes of homelessness.

Attend city council meetings and town halls to voice your concerns and advocate for change. > • Support Organizations: Donate to or volunteer with organizations working to end homelessness and decriminalize poverty.

Challenge negative portrayals of homelessness in the media and engage in respectful conversations with those who hold prejudiced views. > • Show Compassion: Treat homeless individuals with respect and dignity.

A simple act of kindness can make a big difference in someone's day and help to restore their sense of hope and dignity. > By taking these actions, we can create a more just and compassionate society that prioritizes the well-being of all its members, including those experiencing homelessness. > Conclusion > The criminalization of homelessness is a harmful and ineffective approach that exacerbates the challenges faced by those living on the streets.

However, **you generally cannot order your **first** Florida ID card entirely by mail**—you must **visit a Florida Department of Highway Safety and Motor Vehicles (FLHSMV) office in person** to establish identity, residency, and legal presence if this is your first time applying. ### Here's the breakdown: #### **To Get a First-Time State ID in Florida:** You must apply **in person** and bring original documents showing: 1. **Proof of identity** (e.g., birth certificate or valid U.S. passport) 2. **Proof of Social Security Number** (e.g., Social Security card or W-2) 3. **Two documents showing Florida residential address** (e.g., utility bill, lease, letter from a shelter or government agency) #### **If You Already Have a Florida ID:** You **can renew or replace it online or by mail** under certain conditions.

You must have: - No major changes (name change, address change, etc.) - A digital photo on file - A Social Security Number on file with FLHSMV You can check eligibility and order online at: [https://mydmvportal.flhsmv.gov](https://mydmvportal.flhsmv.gov) #### **Homeless or Transitional?** If you're experiencing homelessness: - **You still need to apply in person**, but some exceptions and assistance are available. - **A letter from a shelter or social service provider** can serve as proof of address.

Do they do that for a client like us? **Assistant (2025-07-15 15:15:16 UTC):** **Answer (Structured – Legal & Anecdotal Record):** --- ### ✅ **1.

Lawyers *can and do* represent homeless clients and help them get into housing, temporary shelters, or hotels, **especially through legal avenues like civil rights claims, tenant protection, social services advocacy, or settlement terms**. --- ### 🔍 Historical and Anecdotal Precedents: #### ⚖️ **Legal Aid Housing Interventions** - **Legal Aid organizations** (e.g., Legal Aid Society, Homeless Advocacy Project, Inner City Law Center) frequently represent homeless clients to: - Stop illegal evictions - Enforce **right to shelter** laws (where they exist, like in NYC) - Secure **emergency housing** as part of litigation or social services advocacy - Protect access to **benefits**, **SSI**, or **public housing vouchers** - File **civil rights lawsuits** against agencies or landlords violating constitutional rights #### 📍 **Case Examples:** - **_Callahan v.

Carey_** (New York, 1979): Landmark lawsuit filed on behalf of homeless men arguing a constitutional right to shelter under the NY State Constitution.

Resulted in a consent decree forcing NYC to house the homeless. - **LA Alliance for Human Rights v.

City of Los Angeles** (2020–2023): Civil suit demanding the city provide shelter.

Lawyers used litigation to negotiate hotel room placements, shelters, and transitional housing. - **Advocates in San Francisco** (Coalition on Homelessness): Lawyers helped unhoused people sue the city to stop encampment sweeps unless shelter was offered.

Under Florida law a lot more is going on here than a garden-variety trespass.

The fact that the gate was locked and the lot carried clear signage satisfies the “fenced or posted” aggravator built into the statute. citeturn4search4 2 **Damaging the lock and gate – Criminal mischief (§ 806.13).** Willfully damaging someone else’s real property is criminal mischief.

A first violation is a first-degree misdemeanor; a second (or any fence that was containing livestock at the time) is a third-degree felony. citeturn3search0 4 **Removing the “No Trespassing” sign – theft or further criminal mischief.** The sign is personal property; taking or destroying it can be charged as petit theft (§ 812.014) or folded into the criminal-mischief count.

Florida also makes it an offense to tamper with required postings on private land (§ 810.011). citeturn5search0turn4search2 5 **Replacing the lock with his own – constructive dispossession.** Swapping in a new lock so the owner can’t enter is evidence of an intent to exclude the rightful possessor and can support felony charges such as grand theft of the use of real property or, if the lot were considered the curtilage of a structure, burglary (§ 810.02).

Prosecutors sometimes stack this conduct with the mischief and fence-breaking counts. ### Why this matters * **Multiple counts, higher penalties.** A single incident can yield three or four separate charges.

The most serious listed above—felony criminal mischief or felony fence-breaking—carry penalties of up to five years’ prison and a US$5,000 fine, plus restitution. * **Civil liability.** The owner can also sue for trespass to land, conversion, and the cost of repairs; punitive damages are available when the conduct is willful. ### Practical next steps for a property owner 1. **Document everything.** Take date-stamped photos of the cut lock, new lock, tire tracks, and removed sign; keep any video, eyewitness names, and repair invoices. 2. **Make a written police report.** Hand the officer copies of your deed or lease and the photos.

Cite the statutes above to show the conduct is more than “just trespass.” 3. **Send a certified demand letter (or have counsel do so).** Order the trespasser to remove his lock, cease entry, and pay for damage. 4. **Consider parallel civil action or an injunction** if law-enforcement response is slow. --- *I am not your attorney, and this information is for general educational purposes.

For advice on your specific facts, consult a Florida-licensed lawyer experienced in property and criminal law.* > **User (2025-06-14 18:10:33 UTC):** > > Would the owner have to make the police report or can I if I'm not able to locate or contact the owner? **Assistant (2025-06-14 18:10:34 UTC):** Yes, **you can make the police report** even if you're not the property owner—especially if: ### 1. **You witnessed the crime or discovered the damage.** Florida law does **not** require that only the property owner report criminal activity.

Anyone with first-hand knowledge—whether a neighbor, tenant, passerby, or concerned citizen—can file a police report about: - trespass - criminal mischief (cut locks, damaged fence) - theft (of the No Trespassing sign) - tampering with posted notices ### 2. **You're reporting a crime, not asserting ownership.** You're not claiming the property as yours—you’re **alerting police** that someone is interfering with a piece of land that clearly does not belong to them.

That’s well within your rights. --- ### What will likely happen: - Police will take the report and may contact the **owner of record** (they can look it up via property appraiser records). - If the offense is clear (e.g., lock cut, new lock installed, sign removed), they may open a **criminal investigation** even without the owner’s participation. - However, for charges like **criminal mischief or theft**, they may eventually ask the owner to **confirm the loss/damage** or **press charges.** --- ### Tip: Help establish context To increase police action, include in your report: - How you know it’s private property (e.g. signage, fence, previous knowledge) - When you saw the suspect enter, cut the lock, or change it - That the lot was clearly posted or secured - That the person isn’t using it temporarily—they’ve taken control of it If you can, also: - Take photos or video of the altered gate, removed sign, or truck using the shortcut - Provide a vehicle description, license plate, or footage if available --- ### Bonus: You can also report anonymously You can contact: - **CrimeStoppers of Miami-Dade** at 305-471-TIPS - Or file an **online report** with MDPD or local law enforcement This can spur an investigation and a property check—even if the owner can’t be reached yet. --- Let me know if you want a **template for a civilian police report** or **letter to accompany photos.** > **User (2025-06-14 18:11:12 UTC):** > > What about code enforcement?

Should they be contacted as well? **Assistant (2025-06-14 18:11:13 UTC):** Yes—**absolutely contact Code Enforcement** in addition to the police.

In fact, **code enforcement may act faster** than police in many cases like this, especially when it involves illegal use or alteration of land. ### Here’s why Code Enforcement should be involved: --- ### 🔧 1. **Tampering with fences, signage, and locks is a direct code violation** They enforce property maintenance, illegal alterations, and nuisance laws.

Cutting a lock, removing a fence sign, and replacing it with your own lock can violate local ordinances such as: - **Unauthorized use of private property** - **Obstruction of access** - **Removal or defacement of posted notices (e.g., “No Trespassing”)** - **Illegal modification of a fence or gate** - **Unauthorized driveway or right-of-way use** --- ### 🚧 2. **Driving a truck across private land = illegal use / zoning violation** Using a vacant lot as a **private road or shortcut** without permission likely violates zoning, land-use, or stormwater control codes.

If the lot is zoned residential, for example, it can’t be used for vehicle passage or commercial traffic. --- ### 📬 3. **They can cite and fine the violator without the owner's participation** Code officers can issue **violations, citations, or abatement orders** just based on **your evidence and complaint.** You don’t need to be the owner—**you just need to report the unlawful activity.** --- ### ✅ What Code Enforcement can do: - Inspect the property without needing a warrant (if visible from public areas) - Issue a **Notice of Violation** to the trespasser if identified - Place a warning sticker or notice directly on the altered gate - Demand the lock be removed and the original signage replaced - If ignored, escalate to fines or a hearing before a Code Enforcement Board - Notify the legal property owner by mail or certified letter --- ### 🛠️ How to report: - **Call or visit** the local Code Enforcement office (Miami-Dade Code Enforcement: 305-375-3677) - Or use the **311 app or website**: [miamidade.gov/311direct](https://www.miamidade.gov/311direct) - Include: • The **exact address or parcel location** • A **description of the violation** • **Photos** if possible (cut gate, new lock, missing signage, tire marks) • Vehicle or person description if known --- ### 💡 Pro Tip: Ask Code Enforcement to **“flag” the property** for monitoring, especially if it’s a repeat issue or if squatters or rogue drivers are using multiple vacant lots in the area.

Here’s why: --- ### ⚖️ LEGAL BREAKDOWN: #### 🚫 1. **Unauthorized access = Trespass** Even if he owns the fence **on his side**, installing a gate that **opens into someone else’s land**—and then using it—is a form of: - **Trespass** (§ 810.09, Fla.

Stat.) - **Encroachment** (in property law) - **Illegal access modification** (local zoning/municipal code violation) This is especially egregious if: - The adjoining lot is **fenced, posted, or gated** - He’s not the owner, tenant, or licensee - There’s no **public easement** or **shared-use agreement** --- #### 🏚️ 2. **No easement = no right to cross** Florida property law is clear: **easements** (e.g., for driveways, utilities, access) must be: - In writing - Recorded or provable - Voluntarily granted by the true owner Without that, this “shortcut” is **100% unauthorized**—**especially if he’s driving vehicles across it**. --- #### 🛠️ 3. **Gate installation may violate building codes or fence ordinances** Most municipalities require: - Permits for fences and gates - Setbacks from property lines - No obstruction or alterations to **another person’s land access** Adding a gate without authorization—especially if it facilitates illegal entry—is a **code violation** and may even be considered **criminal mischief** if damage occurred during installation. --- ### 📌 In summary: | Action | Legal? It is, in fact, **deeply conservative**—in the truest sense of conserving the rule of law.

That justice is not for the injured, but for the punctual.
